<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
By a \emph{semilattice} we mean a meet-semilattice with top.
  Every semilattice $(X, \top, \wedge)$ has an underlying partial
  order $\leq$ given by $x \leq y$ iff $x \wedge y = x$.
  A \emph{(semilattice) homomorphism} from $(X, \top, \wedge)$ to
  $(X', \top', \wedge')$ is a function $f : X \to X'$ such that
  $f(\top) = \top'$ and $f(x \wedge y) = f(x) \wedge' f(y)$ for all
  $x, y \in X$.
  We write $\mathsf{MSL}$ for the category of semilattices and homomorphisms.
  
  Similarly, by a \emph{lattice} we mean a bounded lattice,
  and lattice homomorphisms are assumed to preserve these bounds.
  We write $\mathsf{Lat}$ for the category of lattices and lattice homomorphisms.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
A \emph{filter} $p$ of a semilattice $(X, \top, \wedge)$ is a nonempty
  upset $p \subseteq X$ that is closed under meets.
  It is called \emph{principal} if
  $p = {\uparrow} x$ for some $x \in X$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:dual-adj-MSL}
  The functor $\mathcal{F}$ then establishes a dual adjunction between
  $\mathsf{MSL}$ and $\mathsf{MSL}$ with both units given by $\eta$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:L-mor}
  An \emph{L-morphism} between semilattices $(X, \wedge)$ and $(X', \wedge')$
  is a semilattice homomorphism $f : (X, \wedge) \to (X', \wedge')$ 
  that satisfies for all $x \in X$ and $y', z' \in X'$:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item If $f(x) = \top'$ then $x = \top$;
    \item If $y' \wedge z' \leq f(x)$ then $\exists y, z \in X$ s.t.~ 
          $y' \leq' f(y)$ and $z' \leq' f(z)$ and $y \wedge z \leq x$.
          In a picture:
  $$
    \begin{tikzcd}[scale=.95]
        
        & [-1.3em]
          x \arrow[rrrd, "f"]
            \arrow[dddd, dashed, -]
        & [-1.4em]
        & [-1em]
        & [-1.5em]
        & [-1.5em] \\ [-2.5em]
        
        &
        &
        &
        & f(x)
        & \\ [-2.2em]
      y     \arrow[ddr, dashed, -]
        &
        & z \arrow[ddl, dashed, -]
            \arrow[rrrd, bend left=-2, dashed, "f"]
        &&& \\ [-2.1em]
        
        &
        &
        & f(y) \arrow[dd, dashed, -]
               \arrow[lllu, bend left=3, dashed, <-, crossing over, "f" pos=.25]
        &
        & f(z) \arrow[dd, dashed, -] \\ [-2.6em]
        
        & y \wedge z
        &&&& \\ [-1.7em]
        
        &
        &
        & y' \arrow[dr, -]
        &
        & z' \arrow[dl, -] \\ [-1.5em]
        
        &
        &
        &
        & y' \wedge z'  \arrow[uuuuu, crossing over, -]
        &
    \end{tikzcd}
  $$
  \end{itemize}
  We write $\mathsf{LFrm}$ for the category of semilattices and L-morphisms.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:Lmor-latmor}
  If $f : (X, \top, \wedge) \to (X', \top', \wedge')$ is an L-morphism,
  then $f^{-1} : \mathcal{F}(X', \top', \wedge') \to \mathcal{F}(X, \top, \wedge)$ is a lattice
  homomorphism.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
We know that $f^{-1}$ is a semilattice homomorphism.
  The map $f^{-1}$ preserves the bottom element because
  $f^{-1}(\{ \top' \}) = \{ \top \}$.
 
  For preservation of joins, we need to show that
  \begin{equation}
    f^{-1}(a' \mathbin{\triangledown} b') = f^{-1}(a') \mathbin{\triangledown} f^{-1}(b')
  \end{equation}
  for $a', b' \in \mathcal{F}(X', \top', \wedge')$.
  The inclusion $\supseteq$ follows from the fact that
  $f^{-1}(a' \mathbin{\triangledown} b')$ is a filter that contains both $f^{-1}(a')$ and
  $f^{-1}(b')$. Conversely, if $x \in f^{-1}(a' \mathbin{\triangledown} b')$ then
  $f(x) \in a' \mathbin{\triangledown} b'$ so there exist $y' \in a'$ and $z' \in b'$
  such that $y' \wedge z' \leq f(x)$.
  Since $f$ is an L-frame morphism, we can find $y, z \in X$ such that
  $y' \leq f(y)$ and $z' \leq f(z)$ and $y \wedge z \leq x$.
  This means that $y \in f^{-1}(a')$ and $z \in f^{-1}(b')$, and hence
  $x \in f^{-1}(a') \mathbin{\triangledown} f^{-1}(b')$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:latmor-Lmor}
  Let $h : L \to L'$ be a lattice homomorphism.
  Then $h^{-1} : \mathcal{F} L' \to \mathcal{F} L$ is an L-morphism.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We know that $h^{-1}$ is a semilattice homomorphism,
  so we only have to show that it satisfies the additional conditions
  from Definition~\ref{def:L-mor}.
 
  For the first one, suppose $h^{-1}(p') = L$ ($L$ is the top element of $\mathcal{F} L$).
  Then $\bot \in h^{-1}(p')$, so $\bot' = h(\bot) \in p'$, and therefore
  $p' = L'$.
  
  Next, let $p' \in \mathcal{F} L'$ and $q, r \in \mathcal{F} L$ and suppose
  $q \cap r \subseteq h^{-1}(p')$.
  Let $q' := {\uparrow}h[q]$ and $r' := {\uparrow} h[r]$.
  Then it is easy to verify that $q'$ and $r'$ are filters (because $q$ and $r$ are),
  and by construction $q \subseteq h^{-1}(q')$ and $r \subseteq h^{-1}(r')$.
  It remains to show that $q' \cap r' \subseteq p'$.
 
  Let $a' \in L'$ be such that $a' \in q' \cap r'$.
  Since $a' \in q'$ there exists $a \in q$ such that $h(a) \leq a'$.
  Since $a' \in r'$ there exists $b \in r$ such that $h(b) \leq a'$.
  But then $a \vee b \in q \cap r$, so by assumption $h(a \vee b) \in p'$.
  This implies $a' \in p'$, because
  $h(a \vee b) = h(a) \vee h(b) \leq a'$ and $p'$ is a filter (hence up-closed).
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
An \emph{HMS-space} is a tuple $\mathbb{X} = (X, \top, \wedge, \tau)$
  such that
  $(X, \top, \wedge)$ is a semilattice,
  $(X, \tau)$ is a compact topological space,
  and $\mathbb{X}$ satisfies the \emph{HMS separation axiom}:
  \begin{center}
    for all $x, y \in X$, if $x \not\leq y$ then there exists a clopen
    filter $a$ such that $x \in a$ and $y \notin a$;
  \end{center}
  An \emph{HMS-morphism} is a continuous semilattice homomorphism.
  We write $\mathsf{HMS}$ for the category of HMS-spaces and HMS-morphisms.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, \top, \wedge, \tau)$ be an HMS-space and
  $c \subseteq X$ a filter. Then
  (i) $c$ is a closed iff
  (ii) $c$ is principal iff
  (iii) $c$ is the intersection of clopen filters.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The implication (ii) $\Rightarrow$ (iii) follows from the HMS separation
  axiom and (iii) $\Rightarrow$ (i) is obvious.
  For (i) $\Rightarrow$ (ii), suppose $c$ is not principal.
  Then for each $x \in c$ there exists a $y \in c$ strictly below $x$.
  So for each $x \in c$, using the HMS separation axiom, we can find
  a clopen filter $a_x$ such that $c \not\subseteq a_x$.
  Then $\{ a_x \mid x \in c \}$ is an open cover of $c$ without
  finite subcover.
  (Indeed, for every finite collection $a_{x_1}, \ldots, a_{x_n}$
  we can find $y_1, \ldots, y_n$ such that $y_i \in c$ but $y_i \notin a_i$.
  Then $y_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge y_n$ is in $c$ but not in any of the $a_{x_i}$.)
  So $c$ is not compact, hence not closed.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:closed-upward-closure}
  Let $c$ be a closed subset of an M-space $\mathbb{X} = (X, \wedge, \tau)$.
  Then ${\uparrow}c$ is closed as well.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
If $y \notin {\uparrow}c$ then for each $x \in c$ we have
  $x \not\leq y$, hence a clopen filter
  $a_x$ containing $x$ but not $y$. Then $c \subseteq \bigcup_{x \in c} a_x$,
  so by compactness we find a finite subcover, say,
  $c \subseteq a_1 \cup \cdots \cup a_n$.
  Since all the $a_i$ are upward closed, we have
  ${\uparrow}c \subseteq a_1 \cup \cdots \cup a_n$.
  By construction, none of the $a_i$ contain $y$, so
  $X \setminus (a_1 \cup \cdots \cup a_n)$ is an open neighbourhood of
  $y$ disjoint from ${\uparrow}c$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
Let $A$ be a bounded semilattice.
  Define $\mathcal{F}_{top} A = (\mathcal{F} A, A, \cap, \tau_A)$,
  where $\tau_A$ is the topology generated by
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:sl-mspace-subbase}
    \{ \theta_A(a) \mid a \in A \} \cup \{ \theta_A(a)^c \mid a \in A \},
  \end{equation}
  with $\theta_A(a) = \{ p \in \mathcal{F} A \mid a \in p \}$
  and $\theta_A(a)^c = \mathcal{F} A \setminus \theta_A(a)$.
  Defining $\mathcal{F}_{top} h = h^{-1}$ for a semilattice homomorphism $h$,
  we obtain a contravariant functor
  $
    \mathcal{F}_{top} : \mathsf{MSL} \to \mathsf{HMS}.
  $
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:mspace-bsl}
  The functors $\mathcal{F}_{clp}$ and $\mathcal{F}_{top}$ establish a dual equivalence
  $\mathsf{HMS} \equiv^{\operatorname{op}} \mathsf{MSL}$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
A \emph{lattice space} or \emph{L-space} is an HMS-space
  $\mathbb{X} = (X, \top, \wedge, \tau)$ such that $a \mathbin{\triangledown} b$
  is clopen whenever $a$ and $b$ are clopen filters.
  An L-space morphism is a continuous L-morphism.
  We write $\mathsf{LSpace}$ for the category of L-spaces and their morphisms.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:duality-lat-lspace}
  The duality for bounded semilattices from Theorem~\ref{thm:mspace-bsl}
  restricts to a duality
  $$
    \mathsf{LSpace} \equiv^{\operatorname{op}} \mathsf{Lat}.
  $$
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We only have to verify that the restriction of $\mathcal{F}_{clp}$ to $\mathsf{LSpace}$ lands in
  $\mathsf{Lat}$, and the restriction of $\mathcal{F}_{top}$ to $\mathsf{Lat}$ lands in $\mathsf{LSpace}$.
 
                                                                     
                                            
                                        
  The former follows from the fact that the clopen filters of an L-space are
  closed under $\mathbin{\triangledown}$, together with Proposition~\ref{prop:Lmor-latmor}.

  For the latter, suppose that $L$ is a lattice and let $\theta_L(a)$ and
  $\theta_L(b)$ be two arbitrary clopen filters of $\mathcal{F}_{top} L$.
                                                                   
  Writing $x, y, z$ for elements in $\mathcal{F}_{top} L$, we have
  $$
    \theta_L(a) \mathbin{\triangledown} \theta_L(b)
      = {\uparrow} \{ x \cap y \mid x \in \theta_L(a), y \in \theta_L(b) \}
      = \theta_L(a \vee b)
  $$
  Let us elaborate on the last equality.
                                                                                          
  If $x \in \theta_L(a)$ and $y \in \theta_L(b)$ then
  $a \in x$ and $b \in y$, so $a \vee b \in x \cap y$.
  So $z \supseteq x \cap y$ implies $a \vee b \in z$, and therefore we have
  ``$\subseteq$''.
  Conversely, if $z \in \theta_L(a \vee b)$ then we need to find
  $x \in \theta_L(a)$ and $y \in \theta_L(b)$ such that $x \cap y \subseteq z$.
  Let $x = {\uparrow}a \in \theta_L(a)$ and $y = {\uparrow}b \in \theta_L(b)$. 
  Then $d \in x \cap y$ implies $a \leq d$ and $b \leq d$, hence
  $a \vee b \leq d$. Since $z \in \theta_L(a \vee b)$ this implies $d \in z$,
  and therefore $x \cap y \subseteq z$. This proves ``$\supseteq$''.
  The restriction on morphisms follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:latmor-Lmor}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
A \emph{completion} of a lattice $L$ is a pair $(e, C)$ where $C$ is a
  complete lattice and $e : L \to C$ is a lattice embedding.
  An element in $C$ is called \emph{open} if it is the join of elements in the
  image of $e$, and \emph{closed} if it is the meet of elements in the image of
  $e$.
  
  A completion $(e, C)$ is called \emph{dense}
  if every element of $C$ can be written as the join of meets of elements
  in $L$, and as the meet of joins of elements in $L$.
  It is called \emph{compact} if for any set $A$ of closed elements of $C$
  and $B$ of open elements of $C$, $\bigwedge A \leq \bigvee B$ if and
  only if there are finite subsets $A' \subseteq A$ and $B' \subseteq B$
  such that $\bigwedge A' \leq \bigvee B'$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
Let $L$ be a lattice.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item The \emph{ideal completion} of $L$ is the collection $\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}} L$ of
          ideals of $L$ ordered by inclusion, with $i : L \to \operatorname{\mathcal{ie}} L$ given
          by $a \mapsto {\downarrow}a$.
          Meets in $\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}} L$ are given by intersection.
          As a consequence, the join of a collection of ideals is the smallest
          ideal containing their union.
    \item The \emph{filter completion} of $L$ is the collection
          $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L$ of filters of $L$ ordered by reverse inclusion,
          with $i : L \to \operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L : a \mapsto {\uparrow}a$.
          Then arbitrary joins in $\mathcal{F}L$ are given by intersections,
          and the meet of a collection of filters in $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L$ is the
          smallest filter of $L$ containing their union.
    \item The \emph{canonical extension} of $L$ is the unique dense and
          compact completion of $L$.
    \item The \emph{$\Pi_1$-completion} of a lattice $L$ is given by
          the composition of the ideal and the filter completion.
          That is, it consists of the lattice $\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L)$
          with inclusion $a \mapsto \{ p \in \operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L \mid a \in p \}$.
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:lattice-completions}
  Let $L$ be a lattice and $\mathbb{X}_L$ its dual L-space.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item The filter completion of $L$ is isomorphic to the
          complete lattice $\mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}_L)$ of principal filters of
          $\mathbb{X}_L$ of $L$, with inclusion
          $\theta_L: L \to \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}_L) : a \mapsto \theta(a)$.
    \item The canonical extension of $L$ is isomorphic to the
          complete lattice $\mathcal{F}_{sat}(\mathbb{X}_L)$ of saturated filters of
          $\mathbb{X}_L$, with inclusion
          $\theta_L : L \to \mathcal{F}_{sat}(\mathbb{X}_L) : a \mapsto \theta(a)$.
    \item The $\Pi_1$-completion of $L$ is isomorphic to the
          complete lattice $\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{X}_L)$ of filters of $\mathbb{X}_L$,
          with inclusion $\theta_L : L \to \mathcal{F}(\mathbb{X}_L) : a \mapsto \theta(a)$.
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The first item follows from the lattice of principal filters of $L$ being isomorphic to $L^\circ$ and $\mathcal{F} L = (\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L)^o$. The second item is similar to~\cite[Theorem~4.1]{MosJip09}. Finally, the third item follows from the mentioned above connection between filter and ideal completions $\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}} L = (\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L^\circ)^\circ$ together with $\mathcal{F} L = (\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}} L)^o$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def:logic}
  Let $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}$ be the smallest set of consequence pairs
  closed under the following axioms and rules:
  \begin{align*}
    p \trianglelefteqslant \top,
      &\qquad \bot \trianglelefteqslant p,
      &\text{\emph{top} and \emph{bottom}} \\
    p \trianglelefteqslant p,
      &\qquad \frac{p \trianglelefteqslant q \quad q \trianglelefteqslant r}{p \trianglelefteqslant r},
      &\text{\emph{reflexivity} and \emph{transitivity}} \\
    p \wedge q \trianglelefteqslant p,
       \qquad p \wedge q \trianglelefteqslant q,
      &\qquad \dfrac{r \trianglelefteqslant p \quad r \trianglelefteqslant q}{r \trianglelefteqslant p \wedge q},
      &\text{\emph{conjunction rules}} \\
    p \trianglelefteqslant p \vee q,
       \qquad q \trianglelefteqslant p \vee q,
      &\qquad \dfrac{p \trianglelefteqslant r \quad q \trianglelefteqslant r}{p \vee q \trianglelefteqslant r}
      &\text{\emph{disjunction rules}}
  \end{align*}
 
  If $\Gamma$ is a set of consequence pairs then we let
  $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}(\Gamma)$ denote the smallest set of consequence pairs
  closed under uniform substitution, the axioms and rules mentioned above and those in $\Gamma$.
 
  We write $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi \in \boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}(\Gamma)$
  and $\varphi \dashv\vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if both $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$
  and $\psi \vdash_{\Gamma} \varphi$.
                                           
                                                                      
                                            
  If $\Gamma$ is the empty set then we simply write
  $\varphi \vdash \psi$ and $\varphi \dashv\vdash \psi$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
Let $A$ be a lattice with operations $\top_A, \bot_A, \wedge_A, \vee_A$,
  and induced order $\leq_A$.
  A \emph{lattice model} is a pair $\mathscr{A} = (A, \sigma)$ 
  consisting of a lattice $A$ and an assignment $\sigma : \operatorname{Prop} \to A$
  of the proposition letters.
  The assignment $\sigma$ uniquely extends to a map
  $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \cdot \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}} : \mathbf{L} \to A$
  by interpreting connectives with their lattice counterparts.
  
                               
                                                            
                               
                 
                                       
                                           
                                            
                                                                         
       
        
                                          
                                          
                                                                     
               
  We say that a lattice $A$ \emph{validates} a consequence pair $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
  if $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}} \leq_A \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}}$ for
  all lattice models $\mathscr{A}$ based on $A$, notation: $A \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
  If $\Gamma$ is a set of consequence pairs then we write
  $\mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)$ for the full subcategory of $\mathsf{Lat}$ whose objects
  validate all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label{def:alg-entail}
  Let $\Gamma \cup \{ \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi \}$ be a set of consequence pairs.
  Write $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\Gamma}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$ if 
  $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}} \leq_A \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}}$ for
  every lattice model $\mathscr{A} = (A, \sigma)$ with $A \in \mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)$.
  We abbreviate $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\emptyset}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$
  to $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:LT}
  We have $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\Gamma}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$ if and only if
  $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}} \leq_L \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}}$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The ``only if'' holds by definition.
  Conversely, if $A \in \mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)$ and $\mathscr{A} = (A, \sigma_A)$
  is a lattice model, then the assignment $[p] \mapsto \sigma_A(p)$
  extends to a lattice homomorphism $i : \mathscr{L}_{\Gamma} \to \mathscr{A}$
  such that $[\varphi] = \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}}$.
  (This is well defined because $A$ validates all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$.)
  Then $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}} \leq_L \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}}$
  implies $[\varphi] \leq_L [\psi]$. Monotonicity of $i$ yields
  $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}} \leq_L \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{A}}$,
  hence~$\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\Gamma}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:alg-sem}
  We have $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if and only if $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\Gamma}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:LT} it suffices to show that
  $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if and only if
  $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}} \leq_L \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}}$.
 
  It follows from the conjunction rules, reflexivity and transitivity that
  $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if and only if
  $\varphi \wedge \psi \dashv\vdash_{\Gamma} \varphi$.
  Therefore we have $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if and only if
  $[\varphi \wedge \psi] = [\varphi]$ in $\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}$,
  and since $[\varphi \wedge \psi] = [\varphi] \wedge_L [\psi]$ this holds
  if and only if $[\varphi] \leq_L [\psi]$.
  Recalling that $[\varphi] = \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathscr{L}_{\Gamma}}$ completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
\label{def:interpretation}
  A \emph{lattice model} or \emph{L-model} is a semilattice
  $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$ together with a valuation
  $V : \operatorname{Prop} \to \mathcal{F}(X, 1, \curlywedge)$ which assigns to each proposition
  letter a filter of $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$.
 
  An L-model $(X, 1, \curlywedge, V)$ is called \emph{principal} if
  $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$ has a bottom element $0$ and binary joins denoted by
  $\curlyvee$ (so it forms a lattice)
  and $V(p)$ is a principal filter for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.


  The interpretation of an $\mathbf{L}$-formula $\varphi$ at a state $x$ in
  a (principal) L-model $\mathfrak{M} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, V)$ is defined recursively via
  \begin{align*}
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \top &\phantom{\quad\text{iff}\quad} \text{always} \\
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \bot &\quad\text{iff}\quad x = 1 \\
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash p &\quad\text{iff}\quad x \in V(p) \\
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \wedge \psi
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \text{ and } \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \psi \\
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \vee \psi
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \exists y, z \in X \text{ s.t. } \mathfrak{M}, y \Vdash \varphi \text{ and }
            \mathfrak{M}, z \Vdash \psi \text{ and } y \curlywedge z \preccurlyeq x
  \end{align*}
  We write $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} := \{ x \in X \mid \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \}$
  for the \emph{truth set} of $\varphi$ in $\mathfrak{M}$.
  If the underlying (semi)lattice is fixed and we want to emphasise the role of the valuation
  in the interpretation, we will write $V(\varphi)$ instead of $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$.
  The \emph{theory} of $x$ is denoted by
  $\operatorname{th}_{\mathfrak{M}}(x) := \{ \varphi \in \mathbf{L} \mid \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \}$.
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
We write $\mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ if $x \in \llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$
  implies $x \in \llbracket \psi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$, and
  $\mathfrak{M} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ if
  $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} \subseteq \llbracket \psi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$.
  If $\mathfrak{X}$ is an L-frame, we let $\mathfrak{X}, x \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ if
  $\mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ for all L-models $\mathfrak{M}$ based on $\mathfrak{X}$,
                          
                                                      
  and $\mathfrak{X} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ if $\mathfrak{X}, x \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ for
  all states $x$ of $\mathfrak{X}$.

  We say that $\mathfrak{M}$ or $\mathfrak{X}$ \emph{validates} $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
  if $\mathfrak{M} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ or $\mathfrak{X} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$, respectively.
 
  If $\Gamma$ is a set of consequence pairs, then we let
  $\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)$ denote the full subcategory of $\mathsf{LFrm}$ whose
  objects validate all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$.
  We write $\varphi \Vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if $\mathfrak{X} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
  for all $\mathfrak{X} \in \mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)$.
  If $\Gamma = \emptyset$ then we write $\varphi \Vdash \psi$ instead of
  $\varphi \Vdash_{\emptyset} \psi$.
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:complex-alg1}
  For every L-model $\mathfrak{M}$ and $\mathbf{L}$-formula $\varphi$ we have
  $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^*}$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
Let $\mathfrak{M} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, V)$ be a (principal) L-model.
  Then for each $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}$ the truth set $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$
  of $\varphi$ is a (principal) filter of $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The fact that $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$ is a filter for each $\varphi$
  follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:complex-alg1}.
  Suppose $\mathfrak{M}$ is principal.
  Then $\llbracket p \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$ is principal by definition,
  as are $\llbracket \bot \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = {\uparrow}\top$
  and $\llbracket \top \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = {\uparrow} 0$.
  If $\varphi = \varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2$ or $\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2$ then
  we proceed by induction.
  We may assume that
  $\llbracket \varphi_1 \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = {\uparrow}x_1$ and
  $\llbracket \varphi_2 \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = {\uparrow}x_2$, so that
  $\llbracket \varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2 \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = {\uparrow}(x_1 \curlyvee x_2)$
  and $\llbracket \varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2 \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = {\uparrow}(x_1 \curlywedge x_2)$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:soundness}
  If $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ then $\varphi \Vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
If $\mathfrak{M}$ is an L-model that validates all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$,
  then $\mathfrak{M}^* \in \mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)$.
  Since $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$, Theorem~\ref{thm:alg-sem} yields
  $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\Gamma}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$, and hence
  $\llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^*} \leq \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \psi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^*}$.
  Lemma~\ref{lem:complex-alg1} now implies
  $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} \leq \llbracket \psi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$,
  so that $\mathfrak{M}$ validates $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
An \emph{L-model morphism} from
  $(X, 1, \curlywedge, V)$ to $(X', 1', \curlywedge', V')$ is
  an L-morphism (Definition~\ref{def:L-mor})
  $f : (X, 1, \curlywedge) \to (X', 1', \curlywedge')$
  that satisfies $V = f^{-1} \circ V'$.
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
Let $f : \mathfrak{M} \to \mathfrak{M}'$ be an L-model morphism.
  Then for all states $x$ of $\mathfrak{M}$ and all $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}$,
  $$
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \quad\text{iff}\quad \mathfrak{M}', f(x) \Vdash \varphi.
  $$
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- definition-15: body_md -->
\label{def:inerpretation-lspace}
  A \emph{clopen valuation} for an L-space $\mathbb{X}$ is an assignment
  $V : \operatorname{Prop} \to \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}$, which assigns to each proposition letter
  a clopen filter of $\mathbb{X}$. We call a pair $\mathbb{M} = (\mathbb{X}, V)$
  of an L-space and a clopen valuation an \emph{L-space model}.
 
  The interpretation $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathbb{M}}$ of an $\mathbf{L}$-formula
  $\varphi$ in an L-space model $\mathbb{M} = (\mathbb{X}, V)$ is defined as in
  the underlying L-model $(\kappa\mathbb{X}, V)$.

  An L-space model $\mathbb{M}$ validates a consequence pair $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
  if $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathbb{M}} \subseteq \llbracket \psi \rrbracket^{\mathbb{M}}$,
  notation: $\mathbb{M} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
                                     
  We say that an L-space $\mathbb{X}$ validates $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ if
  every L-space model based on it validates $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
  Finally, we write $\varphi \Vdash_{\mathsf{LSpace}} \psi$ if every L-space
  validates $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
<!-- end definition-15 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:Lspace-lattice-val}
  Let $\mathbb{X}$ be an L-space, $A$ its dual lattice, and $\varphi, \psi \in \mathbf{L}$.
  Then
  $$
    \mathbb{X} \Vdash \varphi \text{ iff } A \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi
    \quad\text{and}\quad
    \mathbb{X} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi \text{ iff } A \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi.
  $$
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The first ``iff'' follows from the fact that clopen valuations of $\mathbb{X}$
  correspond bijectively to assignments of the proposition letters for $A$,
  together with a routine induction on the structure of $\varphi$.
  The second ``iff'' follows immediately from the first.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- definition-16: body_md -->
A consequence pair $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ is called \emph{$\Pi_1$-persistent} if
  for every L-space $\mathbb{X}$,
  $$
    \mathbb{X} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi
    \quad\text{implies}\quad
    \kappa\mathbb{X} \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi.
  $$
<!-- end definition-16 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:canonicity}
  Any consequence pair $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ of $\mathbf{L}$-formulae is
  $\Pi_1$-persistent.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:compl}
  Let $\Gamma$ be a set of consequence pairs.
  Then the logic $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}(\Gamma)$ is sound and complete with respect to the
  following classes of frames:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathsf{D\hyphen LFrm}(\Gamma)$ $($descriptive frames validating $\Gamma)$;
    \item $\mathsf{PLFrm}(\Gamma)$ $($principal L-frames validating $\Gamma)$;
    \item $\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)$ $($L-frames validating $\Gamma)$.
  \end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Soundness holds by definition, so we prove completeness.
  Suppose $\varphi \not\vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$.
  Then by Theorem~\ref{thm:alg-sem} we can find a lattice $A$
  validating all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$, but not $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
  As a consequence of Lemma~\ref{lem:Lspace-lattice-val} the L-space
  $\mathbb{X}$ dual to $A$ validates all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$
  but does not validate $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$, thus we find completeness
  with respect to $\mathsf{D\hyphen LFrm}(\Gamma)$.
  
  Since $\mathbb{X} \not\Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
  there must exist a clopen valuation $V$ such that
  $(\mathbb{X}, V) \not\Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
  Therefore $(\kappa\mathbb{X}, V) \not\Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$.
  Besides, Lemma~\ref{lem:canonicity} implies that
  $\kappa\mathbb{X}$ validates all consequence pairs in $\Gamma$.
  This implies completeness with respect to $\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)$.
 
  Lastly, we note that $\mathbb{X}$ is a principal L-frame and
  since $V$ is clopen is assigns to each proposition letter a principal filter.
  Thus $\mathbb{X}$ is a principal L-frame validating $\Gamma$ but not
  $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$, proving completeness with respect to
  $\mathsf{PLFrm}(\Gamma)$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- definition-17: body_md -->
Let $\mathbf{FOL}$ be the single-sorted first-order language which has a
  unary predicate $P_p$ for every proposition letter $p$,
  and a binary relation symbol $R$.
<!-- end definition-17 -->
<!-- definition-18: body_md -->
\label{def:st}
  Let $x$ be a first-order variable.
  Define the standard translation $\operatorname{st}_x : \mathbf{L} \to \mathbf{FOL}$ recursively
  via
  \begin{align*}
    \operatorname{st}_x(p) &= P_px \\
    \operatorname{st}_x(\top) &= (x = x) \\
    \operatorname{st}_x(\bot) &= \forall y(y R x) \\
    \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi \wedge \psi) &= \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi) \wedge \operatorname{st}_x(\psi) \\
    \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi \vee \psi)
      &= \exists y \exists z (\mathsf{abovemeet}(x; y, z)
         \wedge \operatorname{st}_y(\varphi) \wedge \operatorname{st}_z(\psi))
  \intertext{Furthermore, we define the standard translation of a consequence pair
  $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ as}
    \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi) &= \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi) \to \operatorname{st}_x(\psi).
  \end{align*}
<!-- end definition-18 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:model-corr1}
  For every L-model $\mathfrak{M}$ and state $w$ of $\mathfrak{M}$ we have
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $\mathfrak{M}, w \Vdash \varphi$ iff $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi)[w]$;
                                                                                   
    \item $\mathfrak{M}, w \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
          iff $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi)[w]$.
                                        
                                                                       
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The first item follows immediately from the definition of the standard
  translation, and the other item follows from the first one.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- definition-19: body_md -->
\label{def:fil-sot}
  Let $p_1, \ldots, p_n$ be the proposition letters occurring in $\psi$
  and $\chi$, and let $P_1, \ldots, P_n$ denote their corresponding unary
  predicates.
  For each $P_i$, abbreviate
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathsf{isfil}(P_i)
      = \exists w P_iw \wedge \forall x \forall y \forall z(
          (P_iy \wedge P_iz \wedge \mathsf{abovemeet}(x; y, z)) \to P_ix).
  \end{equation*}
  Using this abbreviation, we define the \emph{second order translation}
  of a consequence pair $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ by
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:sot}
    \operatorname{so}(\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi)
      = \forall P_1 \cdots \forall P_n \big(
        (\mathsf{isfil}(P_1) \wedge \cdots \wedge \mathsf{isfil}(P_n) \wedge \operatorname{st}_x(\psi))
        \to \operatorname{st}_x(\chi) \big).
  \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-19 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:sot}
  For all L-frames $\mathfrak{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge)$ and all consequence pairs
  $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ we have
  $$
    \mathfrak{X}, w \Vdash \psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi
      \quad\text{iff}\quad \mathfrak{X}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{so}(\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi)[w].
  $$
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
If $\mathfrak{X}, w \Vdash \psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ then $w \in V(\psi)$
  implies $w \in V(\chi)$
  for every valuation $V$ for $\mathfrak{X}$.
  If any of the $P_i$ is inter\-preted as a subset of $X$ that is not
  a filter, then the implication inside the quantifiers in \eqref{eq:sot}
  is automatically true, because the
  antecedent is false. If all $P_i$ are interpreted as filters,
  then the implication holds because of the assumption.
 
  The converse is similar.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- definition-20: body_md -->
Let $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ be a consequence pair and $\alpha(x)$ a first-order
  formula with free variable $x$. Then we say that $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ and
  $\alpha(x)$ are \emph{local frame correspondents} if for any L-frame
  $\mathfrak{X}$ and any state $w$ we have
  $$
    \mathfrak{X}, w \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi
      \quad\text{iff}\quad \mathfrak{X} \models \alpha(x)[w].
  $$
<!-- end definition-20 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:increasing-val}
  Let $\mathfrak{X}$ be an L-frame and let $V$ and $V'$ be valuations for $\mathfrak{X}$
  such that $V(p) \subseteq V'(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  Then for all $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}$ we have $V(\varphi) \subseteq V'(\varphi)$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:corr}
  Any consequence pair $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ of $\mathbf{L}$-formulae
  locally corresponds to a first-order formula with one free variable.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
We know that $\mathfrak{X}, w \Vdash \psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ if and only if
  $\mathfrak{X}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{so}(\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi)[w]$.
  Our strategy for obtaining a first-order correspondent is to remove all
  second-order quantifiers from the second-order translation.
  We assume that                                              
  no two quantifiers bind the same variable.
  
  If $\psi$ is equivalent to $\top$ then as a consequence of Lemma~\ref{lem:increasing-val}
  $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ is equivalent
  to $\top \trianglelefteqslant \chi'$, where $\chi'$ is obtained
  from $\chi$ by replacing all proposition letters with $\bot$.
  This, in turn, implies that $\operatorname{so}(\top \trianglelefteqslant \chi')$ is a first-order
  correspondent of $\top \trianglelefteqslant \chi$, since the lack of proposition
  letters in $\chi'$ implies that there are no second-order
  quantifiers in $\operatorname{so}(\top \trianglelefteqslant \chi')$.
 
  If $\psi$ is equivalent to $\bot$ then $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ is vacuously valid on all L-frames.
  So we may assume that the antecedent does not use $\top$ or $\bot$.
  
  Let $p_1, \ldots, p_n$ be the propositional variables occurring in
  $\psi$, and write $P_1, \ldots, P_n$ for their corresponding unary predicates.
  We assume that every proposition letter that occurs in $\chi$
  also occurs is $\psi$, for otherwise we may replace it by $\bot$ to
  obtain a formula which is equivalent in terms of validity on frames.
  
  \bigskip\noindent
  {\it Step 1.}
                                                                         
                                                                     
                                                 
 
                    
                 
  Use equivalences of the form
  $$
    (\exists w(\alpha(w)) \wedge \beta) \leftrightarrow \exists w(\alpha(w) \wedge \beta),
    \qquad
    (\exists w (\alpha(w)) \to \beta) \leftrightarrow\forall w(\alpha(w) \to \beta)
  $$
  to pull out all quantifiers that arise in $\operatorname{st}_x(\psi)$.
  Let $Y := \{ y_1, \ldots, y_m \}$ denote the set of (bound) variables that occur
  in the antecedent of the implication from the second-order translation.
  We end up with a formula of the form
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:post-process11}
    \forall P_1 \cdots \forall P_n \forall y_1 \cdots \forall y_m
    \big( (\operatorname{ISFIL} \wedge \operatorname{AT} \wedge \operatorname{REL}) \to \operatorname{st}_x(\chi) \big).
  \end{equation}
  Here $\operatorname{ISFIL} = \mathsf{isfil}(P_1) \wedge \cdots \wedge \mathsf{isfil}(P_n)$,
  $\operatorname{AT}$ is a conjunction of formulae of the form $P_iz$ and
  $\operatorname{REL}$ is a conjunction of formulae of the form $\mathsf{abovemeet}(z; z', z'')$,
  where $z, z', z'' \in Y \cup \{ x \}$.
  
  \bigskip\noindent
  {\it Step 2.} Next we read off minimal instances of the $P_i$ making the
  antecedent true. Intuitively, these correspond to the smallest valuations
  for the $p_i$ making the antecedent true.
 
  For each proposition letter $P_i$, let $P_iy_{i_1}, \ldots, P_iy_{i_k}$ be
  the occurrences of $P_i$ in $\operatorname{AT}$ in the antecedent of \eqref{eq:post-process11}.
  We define the valuation of $p_i$ to be the filter generated
  by the (interpretations of) $y_{i_1}, \ldots, y_{i_k}$.
  Formally,
  $$
    \sigma(P_i) := \lambda u. \mathsf{abovemeet}(u; y_{i_1}, \ldots, y_{i_k}).
  $$
  (If $k = 0$, i.e.~there is no variable $y$ with $P_iy$, then we let
  $\sigma(P_i) = \lambda u.(u \neq u)$.)
 
  Then for each L-model $\mathfrak{M}$ and states $x', y_1', \ldots, y_m'$ in $\mathfrak{M}$
  we have
  $$
    \mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{AT} \wedge \operatorname{REL}[x, y_1', \ldots, y_m']
    \quad\text{implies}\quad
    \mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \forall y (\sigma(P_i)y \to P_iy).
  $$
  
  If we replace each unary predicate $P$ in \eqref{eq:post-process11} with
  $\sigma(P)$, then all conjoints in $\operatorname{ISFIL}$ and $\operatorname{AT}$ become true.
  Writing $[\sigma(P)/P]\operatorname{st}_x(\chi)$ for the formula obtained from $\operatorname{st}_x(\chi)$
  by replacing each instance of a unary predicate $P$ with $\sigma(P)$,
  we arrive at the first-order formula
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sahl-2}
    \forall y_1 \cdots \forall y_m \big(
      \operatorname{REL}
      \to [\sigma(P_i)/P_i]\operatorname{st}_x(\chi) \big)
  \end{equation}
  
  \bigskip\noindent
  {\it Step 3.}
  Finally, we claim that for every L-frame $\mathfrak{X}$,
  $\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}$ validates \eqref{eq:post-process11} if and only if
  it validates \eqref{eq:Sahl-2}.
  The implication from left to right is simply an instantiation of
  the quantifiers as filters.
  For the converse, assume that $\mathfrak{M}$ is some model based on $\mathfrak{X}$,
  so that $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}$ is an extension of $\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}$ giving
  the interpretations of the unary predicates as filters. We may disregard
  the case where any of them is not a filter as that would make the antecedent
  in \eqref{eq:post-process11} false, hence the whole implication true.
  Let $x', y_1', \ldots, y_m'$ be states in $\mathfrak{M}$
  and assume that
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sahl-3}
    \mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{ISFIL} \wedge \operatorname{AT} \wedge \operatorname{REL}[x', y_1', \ldots, y_m'].
  \end{equation}
  We need to show that $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{st}_x(\chi)[x', y_1', \cdots, y_m']$.
  It follows from the assumption that \eqref{eq:Sahl-2} holds that
  $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models [\sigma(P)/P]\operatorname{st}_x(\chi)[x', y_1', \cdots, y_m']$.
  Moreover, as a consequence of \eqref{eq:Sahl-3} we have
  $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \forall y(\sigma(P)(y) \to Py)$ for all
  $P \in \{ P_1, \ldots, P_n \}$.
  Using Lemma~\ref{lem:increasing-val} it follows that
  $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{st}_x(\chi)[x', y_1', \cdots, y_m']$, as desired.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- definition-21: body_md -->
\label{def:modal-l-frame}
  A \emph{modal L-frame} is a tuple $(X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ where
  $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$ is an L-frame with underlying partial order $\preccurlyeq$,
  and $R$ is a binary relation on $X$ such that:
  \begin{enumerate}
                                           
    \item \label{it:mlf-1}
          If $x \preccurlyeq y$ and $yRz$ then there exists a $w \in X$ such that
          $xRw$ and $w \preccurlyeq y$;
    \item \label{it:mlf-2}
          If $x \preccurlyeq y$ and $xRw$ then there exists a $z \in X$ such that
          $yRz$ and $w \preccurlyeq z$;
    \item \label{it:mlf-3}
          If $(x \curlywedge y)Rz$ then there exist $v, w \in X$ such that
          $xRv$ and $yRw$ and $v \curlywedge w \preccurlyeq z$;
    \item \label{it:mlf-4}
                                                               
                                                               
          If $xRv$ and $yRw$ then $(x \curlywedge y)R(v \curlywedge w)$;
                           
                                                                      
                                           
    \item \label{it:mlf-6}
          For all $x$, $1 Rx$ if and only if $x = 1$.
  \end{enumerate}
  A \emph{modal L-model} is a a modal L-frame together with a valuation $V$
  that assigns to each proposition letter a filter of $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$.
  
  Just like in the propositional case in Section~\ref{subsec:l-frame},
  we can identify a class of frames where formulae can be interpreted
  exclusively as \emph{principal} filters.
  To this end, we define a \emph{principal modal L-frame} as a
  modal L-frame $(X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ that additionally satisfies:
  \begin{enumerate}
    \setcounter{enumi}{-1}
    \item \label{it:pmlf-0}
           $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$ has binary joins and all non-empty meets;
    \setcounter{enumi}{2}
    \renewcommand{$\arabic{enumi}'$}{$\arabic{enumi}'$}
    \renewcommand{$\arabic{enumi}'$.}{$\arabic{enumi}'$.}
    \item \label{it:pmlf-3}
                                                                      
                                                   
          If $(\bigcurlywedge x_i)Rz$, where the $i$ range over some index set $I$,
          then there exist $z_i$ such that $x_iRz_i$ for all $i \in I$
          and $\bigcurlywedge z_i \leq z$;
    \item \label{it:pmlf-4}
                                                               
          If $x_iRy_i$, where $i$ ranges over some index set $I$,
          then $(\bigcurlywedge x_i)R(\bigcurlywedge y_i)$;
  \end{enumerate}
  Clearly, items~\eqref{it:pmlf-3} and~\eqref{it:pmlf-4}
  subsume~\eqref{it:mlf-3} and~\eqref{it:mlf-4}.
  A principal modal L-model is a principal modal L-frame with a valuation
  that assigns to each proposition letter a principal filter.
<!-- end definition-21 -->
<!-- definition-22: body_md -->
The interpretation of $\mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$-formulae in a
  (principal) modal L-model $\mathfrak{M}$ is defined via the clauses from
  Definition~\ref{def:interpretation} and
  \begin{align*}
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \forall y \in X, xRy \text{ implies } \mathfrak{M}, y \Vdash \varphi \\
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \exists y \in X \text{ such that } xRy \text{ and } \mathfrak{M}, y \Vdash \varphi
  \end{align*}
  Satisfaction and validity of formulae and modal consequence pairs
  are defined as expected.
 
  In particular, if $\mathscr{K}$ is a class of modal L-frames and $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
  is a modal consequence pair, then we write
  $\varphi \Vdash_{\mathscr{K}} \psi$ if the consequence pair $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ is valid
  on all frames in $\mathscr{K}$.
<!-- end definition-22 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:modal-persistence3}               
  Let $\mathfrak{M} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, R, V)$ be a (principal) modal L-model.
  Then for each $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$ the set
  $\llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$ is a (principal) filter in $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We assume that $\mathfrak{M}$ is not principal; the case for principal modal
  L-models is similar.
  The proof proceeds by induction on the structure of $\varphi$.
  The only non-trivial cases are the modal cases.
  We prove the statement for $\varphi = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$;
  the case $\varphi = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$ is similar.

                                
                                              
                                                 
                                                                          
                                                                              
                                                                         
                                                              
                               
   
                                                          
                                                        
                                               
                                                                          
                            
                                                                              
                                                                         
                                            
                                                     
                                                            
  
  Suppose $\varphi = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$,
  $\mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$ and $x \preccurlyeq y$.
  Then there exists an $R$-successor $z$ of $x$ satisfying $\psi$,
  and by \eqref{it:mlf-2} we can find an $R$-successor $w$ of $y$
  such that $z \preccurlyeq y$. By the induction hypothesis we then find
  $\mathfrak{M}, w \Vdash \psi$ and therefore $\mathfrak{M}, y \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
 
  Next, suppose that both $x$ and $y$ satisfy $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
  Then there exist $v \in R[x]$ and $w \in R[y]$ satisfying $\psi$.
  By \eqref{it:mlf-4} we have $(x \curlywedge y)R(v \curlywedge w)$ and
  by the induction hypothesis $\mathfrak{M}, v \curlywedge w \Vdash \psi$.
  Therefore $\mathfrak{M}, x \curlywedge y \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
  Lastly, \eqref{it:mlf-6} implies $\mathfrak{M}, 1 \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
  We conclude that $\llbracket \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}$ is a filter
  in $(X, 1, \curlywedge)$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- definition-23: body_md -->
\label{def:mlfm}
  A \emph{bounded L-morphism} from $(X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ to
  $(X', 1', \curlywedge', R')$ is an L-morphism
  $f : (X, 1, \curlywedge) \to (X', 1', \curlywedge')$
  such that for all $x, y \in X$ and $z' \in X'$:
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{it:mlfm-1}
          If $xRy$ then $f(x)R'f(y)$;
    \item \label{it:mlfm-2}
          If $f(x)R'z'$ then there exists a $z \in X$ such that
          $xRz$ and $f(z) \preccurlyeq z'$;
    \item \label{it:mlfm-3}
          If $f(x)R'z'$ then there exists a $w \in X$ such that
          $xRw$ and $z' \preccurlyeq' f(w)$.
  \end{enumerate}
  (See also Figure~\ref{fig:mlfm}.)
  A bounded L-morphism between models is bounded L-morphism between
  the underlying frames that preserves and reflects truth of proposition letters.
<!-- end definition-23 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:mor-pres-truth-modal}
  Let $\mathfrak{M} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, R, V)$ and $\mathfrak{M}' = (X', 1', \curlywedge', R', V')$ be
  two (principal) modal L-models. If $f : \mathfrak{M} \to \mathfrak{M}'$ is a bounded L-morphism,
  $x \in X$ and $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$, then
  $$
    \mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \varphi \quad\text{iff}\quad \mathfrak{M}', f(x) \Vdash \varphi.
  $$
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
This follows from a routine induction on the structure of $\varphi$.
  We showcase the modal cases of the proof.
  Suppose $\varphi = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$. It follows immediately from
  Definition~\ref{def:mlfm}\eqref{it:mlfm-1} that
  $\mathfrak{M}', f(x) \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$ implies $\mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
  So suppose $\mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
  If $y'$ is an $R'$-successor of $f(x)$, then there exists some
  $z \in X$ such that $xRz$ and $f(z) \preccurlyeq y'$.
  This implies $\mathfrak{M}, z \Vdash \psi$ and by induction 
  $\mathfrak{M}', f(z) \Vdash \psi$. Persistence then yields
  $\mathfrak{M}', y' \Vdash \psi$. Therefore $\mathfrak{M}', f(x) \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
  
  If $\varphi = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$ Then the preservation from
  left to right follows from Definition~\ref{def:mlfm}\eqref{it:mlfm-1}.
  Conversely, if $\mathfrak{M}', f(x) \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$,
  then there exists a $y' \in X'$ such that $f(x)Ry'$ and $\mathfrak{M}', y' \Vdash \psi$.
  By \eqref{it:mlfm-3} we can find some $w \in X$ such that
  $xRw$ and $y' \preccurlyeq f(w)$. Persistence implies $\mathfrak{M}', f(w) \Vdash \psi$
  and induction yields $\mathfrak{M}, w \Vdash \psi$.
  Therefore $\mathfrak{M}, x \Vdash \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:some-val}
  Let $(X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ be a modal L-frame. The following consequence
  pairs are valid:
  \begin{align*}
    \top &\trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\top
      & \top &\trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\top
      &&                          
      & \text{(modal top)} \\
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex(\varphi \wedge \psi) &\trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi
      & \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi &\trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(\varphi \vee \psi)
      &&& \text{(monotonicity)} \\
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi &\trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex(\varphi \wedge \psi)
      &\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex \varphi \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex \psi &\trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(\varphi \wedge \psi)&& 
      & \text{(normality and duality)}
                                                                   
                                                                             
                         
  \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
All of these follow immediately from the definition of the interpretation
  of $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex$ and $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex$.
  In particular, they do not rely on any of the conditions from 
  Definition~\ref{def:modal-l-frame}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- definition-24: body_md -->
\label{def:modal-logic}
  Let $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$ be the smallest set of modal consequence pairs
  closed under the axioms and rules from Definition~\ref{def:logic},
  and under:
  $$
  \begin{array}{cccr}
    \phantom{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex}\top \trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\top
      &&\top \trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\top
                                               
      &\text{(\emph{modal top})} \\ [.4em]
    \dfrac{\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi}{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi}
      &&\dfrac{\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi}{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi}
      &\text{(\emph{Becker's rules})} \\ [1em]
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi \trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex(\varphi \wedge \psi)
      &&\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\varphi \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\psi \trianglelefteqslant \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(\varphi \wedge \psi)
      &\text{(\emph{linearity} and \emph{duality})}
  \end{array}
  $$
  
  If $\Gamma$ is a set of modal consequence pairs then
  $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}(\Gamma)$ denotes the smallest set of modal consequence pairs
  closed under the axioms and rules above and those in $\Gamma$.
 
  We write $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if
  $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi \in \boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}(\Gamma)$
  and $\varphi \dashv\vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ if both $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$
  and $\psi \vdash_{\Gamma} \varphi$,
  omitting $\Gamma$ if it is empty.
<!-- end definition-24 -->
<!-- definition-25: body_md -->
\label{def:modal-lat}
  A \emph{modal lattice} is a tuple $(A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ consisting of a lattice
  $A$ and two maps $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex : A \to A$ satisfying for all $a, b \in A$:
  \begin{align*}
    \top
      &= \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\top
      &  \top
      &= \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\top
      &               
      & \\           
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a
      &\leq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(a \vee b)
      &\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex(a \wedge b)
      &= \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex b
      &\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex b
      &\leq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(a \wedge b)
                       
                                           
  \end{align*}
  A \emph{modal lattice homomorphism} from $(A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ to
  $(A', \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex', \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex')$ is a lattice homomorphism $h : A \to A'$
  such that $h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a) = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex' h(a)$ and $h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a) = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex' h(a)$
  for all $a \in A$.
 
  We write $\mathsf{MLat}$ for the category of modal lattice and modal lattice
  homomorphisms.
<!-- end definition-25 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:complex-alg}
                        
  Let $\mathfrak{M}$ be a modal L-model, $\mathfrak{N}$ a principal modal L-model,
  and $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$.
  Then
  $$
    \llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}} = \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^{*}}
    \quad\text{and}\quad
    \llbracket \varphi \rrbracket^{\mathfrak{N}} = \llparenthesis\kern.2ex \varphi \kern.2ex\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{N}^{\ddagger}}.
  $$
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:alg-sem2}
  Let $\Gamma \cup \{ \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi \}$ be a set of modal consequence pairs.
  Then $\varphi \vdash_{\Gamma} \psi$ iff $\varphi \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{\Gamma}$}}\kern.1ex \psi$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- definition-26: body_md -->
\label{def:mls}
  A \emph{modal L-space} is a tuple $(X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ such that
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{it:mls-1}
          $(X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau)$ is an L-space;
    \item \label{it:mls-1b}
          $R$ is a binary relation on $X$ such that
          $1 R x$ iff $x = 1$ for all $x \in X$;
                                              
                                                
    \item \label{it:mls-2}
          If $a$ is a clopen filter, then so are $[R]a$ and $\langle R \rangle a$;
    \item \label{it:mls-3}
          \label{it:mls-tight}
          For all $x, y \in X$ we have $xRy$ if and only if for all $a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}$:
          \begin{itemize}
            \item If $x \in [R]a$ then $y \in a$;
            \item If $y \in a$ then $x \in \langle R \rangle a$.
          \end{itemize}
  \end{enumerate}
  Truth and validity in modal L-spaces is defined as usual, using
  clopen valuations.
<!-- end definition-26 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:mls-succ-closed}
  Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ be a modal L-space.
  Then $R[x]$ is closed for all $x \in X$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Suppose $y \notin R[x]$.
  Then there exists a clopen filter $a$ such that either $x \in [R]a$
  and $y \notin a$, or $y \in a$ and $x \notin \langle R \rangle a$.
  In the first case $X \setminus a$ is a clopen neighbourhood
  of $y$ disjoint from $R[x]$. In the second case
  $a$ is a clopen neighbourhood of $y$ disjoint from $R[x]$.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:mls-mlf}
  Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ be a modal L-space.
  Then $(X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ is a principal modal L-frame.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We know that L-spaces have all non-empty meets, and hence also binary
  joins, so \eqref{it:pmlf-0} is satisfied.
  Furthermore,~\eqref{it:mlf-6} is satisfied by definition.
  We verify the other conditions from Definition~\ref{def:modal-l-frame},
  starting with \eqref{it:pmlf-4}.

  \medskip\noindent
  {\it Condition \ref{it:pmlf-4}.} \
  Suppose $x_iRy_i$, where $i$ ranges over some index set $I$.
  If $I = \emptyset$ then this condition states $1 R 1$ which holds by definition,
  so assume that $I \neq \emptyset$.
  By the tightness condition of modal L-spaces, in order to prove
  $(\bigcurlywedge x_i)R(\bigcurlywedge y_i)$ it suffices to show that for all
  clopen filters $a$, $\bigcurlywedge x_i \in [R]a$ implies $\bigcurlywedge y_i \in a$
  and $\bigcurlywedge y_i \in a$ implies $\bigcurlywedge x_i \in \langle R \rangle a$.
  
  First assume $\bigcurlywedge x_i \in [R]a$. Since $[R]a$ is a clopen filter
  and $\bigcurlywedge x_i \preccurlyeq x_j$ for each $j \in I$ we have
  $x_j \in [R]a$, so $R[x_j] \subseteq a$. By assumption $x_jRy_j$,
  so $y_j \in a$ for all $j \in I$. Since $a$ is a clopen filter it is
  principal, hence $\bigcurlywedge y_i \in a$.
 
  Second, suppose $\bigcurlywedge y_i \in a$.
  Then $y_j \in a$ for all $j \in I$,
  which implies $x_j \in \langle R \rangle a$ for all $j \in I$.
  Since $\langle R \rangle a$ is a clopen filter, hence principal,
  we find $\bigcurlywedge x_i \in \langle R \rangle a$.
  
  \medskip\noindent
  {\it Condition \ref{it:mlf-1}.} \
  Let $x \preccurlyeq y$ and $yRz$.
  Suppose towards a contradiction that there exists no $w \in X$
  such that $xRw$ and $w \preccurlyeq z$.
  Let $x' = \bigcurlywedge R[x]$ be the minimal element in $R[x]$
  (which is an $R$-successor of $x$ by \eqref{it:pmlf-4}).
  Then $x' \not\preccurlyeq z$, so we can find a clopen filter $a$ containing
  $x'$ such that $z \notin a$. This implies $R[x] \subseteq a$,
  so that $x \in [R]a$, but $y \notin [R]a$ because
  $yRz$ and $z \notin a$. As $x \preccurlyeq y$ this violates the
  fact that $[R]a$ is a filter.

  \medskip\noindent
  {\it Condition \ref{it:mlf-2}.} \
  Let $x \preccurlyeq y$ and $xRw$. Suppose towards a contradiction that
  there exists no $z \in X$ such that $yRz$ and $w \preccurlyeq z$.
  Then $R[y] \cap {\uparrow}w = \emptyset$. Both $R[y]$ and
  ${\uparrow}z$ are closed, as a consequence of Lemmas~\ref{lem:mls-succ-closed}
  and~\ref{lem:closed-upward-closure} and the fact that singletons in
  a Stone spaces are always closed.
  Therefore we can find a clopen filter $a$ containing ${\uparrow}z$
  which is disjoint from $R[y]$. This implies that
  $x \in \langle R \rangle a$ while $y \notin \langle R \rangle a$.
  Since $x \preccurlyeq y$ this contradicts the fact that $\langle R \rangle a$
  is a filter.
  
  \medskip\noindent
  {\it Condition \ref{it:pmlf-3}.} \
  Finally, let $\{ x_i \mid i \in I \}$ be some collection of
  elements of $X$ and suppose $(\bigcurlywedge x_i)Rz$.
  If $I$ is empty then $\bigcurlywedge x_i = 1$ and
  Definition~\ref{def:mls}\eqref{it:mls-1b} implies $z = 1$,
                            
  and the empty collection witnesses truth of~\eqref{it:pmlf-3}.
 
  So assume $I \neq \emptyset$.
  Since $\bigcurlywedge x_i \preccurlyeq x_j$ for all $j \in I$,
  condition \eqref{it:mlf-2} implies that $R[x_j] \neq \emptyset$ for all
  $j \in I$. As a consequence of \eqref{it:pmlf-4} there is a smallest
  element $z_j := \bigcurlywedge R[x_j]$ in each $R[x_j]$.
  We claim that $\bigcurlywedge z_j \leq z$.
  Suppose not, then there is a clopen filter $a$ containing $\bigcurlywedge z_j$
  such that $z \notin a$. This implies $x_j \in [R]a$ for all $j \in I$,
  but $\bigcurlywedge x_i \notin [R]a$ because $(\bigcurlywedge x_i)Rz$ and $z \notin a$.
  But this contradicts the fact that $[R]a$ is principal filter.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- definition-27: body_md -->
Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ be a modal L-space.
  Then we write $\pi\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ for the underlying
  principal modal L-frame, and $\kappa\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, R)$ for the
  underlying principal modal L-frame regarded as a modal L-frame.
<!-- end definition-27 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:Rc}
  Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ be a modal L-space.
  The for every closed filter $c \in \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X})$,         
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Rc1}
    [R] c
      = \bigcap \big\{ [R] a \mid a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}(\mathbb{X}),
         c \subseteq a \big\}                
    \quad\text{and}\quad
    \langle R \rangle c
      = \bigcap \big\{ \langle R \rangle a \mid a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}(\mathbb{X}), c \subseteq a \big\}.
  \end{equation}
  Furthermore, for every filter $d \in \mathcal{F}(\mathbb{X})$ we have
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Rc3}
    [R]d 
      = \operatorname{\bigtriangledown} \big\{ [R]c \mid c \in \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}), c \subseteq d \big\}
    \quad\text{and}\quad
    \langle R \rangle d 
      = \operatorname{\bigtriangledown} \big\{ \langle R \rangle c \mid c \in \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}), c \subseteq d \big\}.
  \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
The left-to-right inclusion of the first equality follows from the fact
  that $c \subseteq a$ implies $[R]c \subseteq [R]a$.
  For the converse, suppose $x \in \mathbb{X}$ is such that
  $x \notin [R]c$. Then $R[x] \not\subseteq c$, so there is some
  $y \in R[x]$ such that $y \notin c$.
  Since $c$ is a closed filter, it is the intersection of all clopen filters
  that contain it. A routine compactness argument yields a clopen
  filter $b$ containing $c$ such that $y \notin b$.
  Therefore $R[x] \not\subseteq b$, so $x \notin [R]b$
  and hence $x$ is not in the right hand side of the equality.
  
  For the second equality, it suffices to prove the right-to-left inclusion as well.
  Suppose $x \notin \langle R \rangle c$. Then $R[x] \cap c = \emptyset$.
  Since $R[x]$ is closed, we can use compactness to find some clopen filter $a$
  containing $c$ disjoint from $R[x]$, and the argument proceeds as above.
  
  Next, consider~\eqref{eq:Rc3}.
  We know that $c \subseteq d$ implies $[R]c \subseteq [R]d$,
  and since the latter is a filter containing $[R]c$
  for all closed filters $c \subseteq d$, it contains the filter generated
  by them. This proves $\supseteq$.
  Conversely, suppose $x \in [R]d$.
  Then $R[x] \subseteq d$.
  Since $d$ is up-closed we also have ${\uparrow} R[x] \subseteq d$.
  The set ${\uparrow} R[x]$ is closed by Lemmas~\ref{lem:mls-succ-closed}
  and~\ref{lem:closed-upward-closure},
  and it is a filter because $R[x]$ is closed under $\curlywedge$.
  Thus we have found a closed filter $c := {\uparrow} R[x]$ contained in $d$ such that
  $x \in [R]c$. This proves~$\subseteq$.
  
  The right-to-left inclusion of the second equality in~\eqref{eq:Rc3}
  is easy again. For $\subseteq$,
  suppose $x \in \langle R \rangle d$. Then $R[x] \cap d \neq \emptyset$,
  so we can find some $y \in R[x] \cap d$.
  Now ${\uparrow} y$ is a closed filter contained in $d$
  such that $x \in \langle R \rangle({\uparrow} y)$, witnessing $\subseteq$.
