<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- definition-28: body_md -->
A \emph{modal L-space morphism} from $(X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ to
  $(X', 1', \curlywedge', \tau', R')$ is a function $f : X \to X'$ such that
  $f : (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau) \to (X', 1', \curlywedge', \tau')$ is an L-space morphism
  and $f : (X, 1, \curlywedge, R) \to (X', 1', \curlywedge', R')$ is a bounded L-morphism.
  We denote the resulting category by $\mathsf{MLSpace}$.
<!-- end definition-28 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
If $\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R)$ is a modal L-space
  then $(\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}, [R], \langle R \rangle)$ is a modal lattice.
 
  Moreover, if $f : \mathbb{X} \to \mathbb{X}'$ is a modal L-space morphism,
  then
  $$
    \mathcal{F}_{clp} f = f^{-1}
      : (\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}', [R'], \langle R' \rangle)
        \to (\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}, [R], \langle R \rangle)
  $$
  is a modal lattice homomorphism.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The maps $[R], \langle R \rangle$ are functions on $\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}$
  by definition.
  It follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:mls-mlf} and
  Lemma~\ref{lem:some-val} that they satisfy the conditions from
  Definition~\ref{def:modal-lat}.
  
  If $f$ is a modal L-space morphism then in particular it is an
  L-space morphism, so $\mathcal{F}_{clp} f$ is a lattice homomorphism
  from $\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}'$ to $\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}$.
  So we only have to show that $f^{-1}$ preserves the modalities.
  This can be proven in the same way as in
  Proposition~\ref{prop:mor-pres-truth-modal}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- definition-29: body_md -->
Let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice.
  Then we define the binary relation $R_A$ on $\mathcal{F}_b A$ by
  $$
    p R_A q \quad\text{iff}\quad \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p) \subseteq q \subseteq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p).
  $$
<!-- end definition-29 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:modal-lat-2}
  Let $(A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice and $p \in \mathcal{F} A$.
                          
  Then $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$ is a filter in $\mathcal{F} A$ and $pR_A\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
The set $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$ is a filter because $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex : A \to A$ preserves
  conjunctions and the top element.
 
  To show $pR_A\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$ we need to prove
  $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p) \subseteq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p) \subseteq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$.
  The left inclusion is trivial.
  For the right one, suppose $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$.
  Then $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in p$. Besides, $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\top = \top \in p$.
  So the duality axiom implies
  $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex\top \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a \leq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(\top \wedge a) = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in p$,
  hence $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:modal-lat-3}
  Let $(A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice. Then for each
  $a \in A$ we have
  $$
    [R_A]\theta_A(a) = \theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a)
    \quad\text{and}\quad
    \langle R_A \rangle\theta_A(a) = \theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a).
  $$
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Suppose $p \in [R_A]\theta_A(a)$.
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:modal-lat-2} we have $pR_A\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$ and
  by assumption $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$. This implies that $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in p$
  and therefore $p \in \theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a)$.
   
  For the reverse inclusion, suppose $p \in \theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a)$.
  Then $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$, so $pR_Aq$ implies $a \in q$,
  and hence $p \in [R_A]\theta_A(a)$.
  
  Next, suppose $p \in \langle R_A \rangle\theta_A(a)$.
  Then there exists a filter $q$ such that $pR_Aq$ and $a \in q$.
  By definition of $R_A$ this implies $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$ and hence
  $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in p$, so $p \in \theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a)$.
 
  Conversely, suppose $p \in \theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a)$.
  Let $q$ be the filter generated by $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$ and $a$.
  We claim that $c \in q$ implies $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c \in p$.
  To see this, note that for each $c \in q$ there exists some $d \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$
  such that $d \wedge a \leq c$.
  We then have $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex d \in p$, and $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in p$ by assumption,
  so $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex d \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in p$.
  Since $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex d \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \leq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex(d \wedge a) \leq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c$
  we find $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c \in p$.
  The filter $q$ is nonempty because it contains $\top$.
  Furthermore, we have $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p) \subseteq q$ by definition of $q$
  and we just showed that $c \in q$ implies $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c \in p$,
  so that $q \subseteq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(p)$.
  This proves $pR_Aq$.
  By design $a \in q$ so $q$ witnesses the fact that
  $p \in \langle R_A \rangle \theta_A(a)$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
If $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ is a modal lattice, then
  $\mathscr{A}_* := (\mathcal{F} A, A, \cap, \tau_A, R_A)$ is a modal L-space.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
We verify the condition from Definition~\ref{def:mls}.
  Item \eqref{it:mls-1} follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:duality-lat-lspace}.
  Item \eqref{it:mls-1b} follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:modal-lat-2}.
  Item \eqref{it:mls-2} follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:modal-lat-3}.
  Item \eqref{it:mls-3} follows from the definition
  of $R_A$ and the fact that each clopen filter is of the form
  $\theta_A(a)$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
Let $h : \mathscr{A} \to \mathscr{A}'$ be a modal lattice homomorphism.
  Then $\mathcal{F} h = h^{-1} : \mathscr{A}'_* \to \mathscr{A}_*$ is a bounded
  L-space morphism.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
It follows from the duality between lattices and L-spaces that
  $h^{-1}$ is an L-space morphism, so we only have to verify the
  three conditions from Definition~\ref{def:mlfm}.
  We write $R'$ and $R$ for the relations from $\mathscr{A}'_*$ and
  $\mathscr{A}_*$.
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Let $p'$ and $q'$ be filters of $\mathscr{A}'$ (elements of $\mathscr{A}'_*$)
          such that $p'R'q'$. In order to prove that
          $h^{-1}(p')Rh^{-1}(q')$ we have to show that
          $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(h^{-1}(p')) \subseteq h^{-1}(q') \subseteq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(h^{-1}(p'))$.
          Let $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(h^{-1}(p'))$.
          Then $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in h^{-1}(p')$ so $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex'(h(a)) = h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a) \in p'$.
          Therefore $h(a) \in (\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p')$, and since $p'R'q'$ this implies
          $h(a) \in q'$, so that $a \in h^{-1}(q')$.
           
          Next, if $a \in h^{-1}(q')$ then $h(a) \in q'$, so
          $h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a) = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex' h(a) \in p'$.
          Therefore $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in h^{-1}(p')$ so that $a \in \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{-1}(h^{-1}(p'))$.
    \item Suppose $h^{-1}(p')Rq$.
                                                 
                                                                         
           
          Lemma~\ref{lem:modal-lat-2} then implies that $p'R'(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p')$.
           
          So it suffices to show that
          $h^{-1}((\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p')) \subseteq q$.
          To this end, suppose $a \in h^{-1}((\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p'))$.
          Then $h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a) = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex' h(a) \in p'$, so $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a \in h^{-1}(p')$.
          Since $h^{-1}(p')Rq$ this implies $a \in q$, as desired.
    \item Suppose $h^{-1}(p')Rq$. Then ${\uparrow}h[q]$ is a filter
          (since $h$ is a lattice homomorphism it is non-empty and closed under meets).
          Let $q'$ be the filter generated by ${\uparrow}h[q]$ and
          $(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p')$. Then $q \subseteq h^{-1}(q')$ by construction,
          so it suffices to show that $p'R'q'$.
          We have $(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p') \subseteq q'$ by definition,
          so we only have to show that $q' \subseteq (\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p')$.
          Let $a' \in q'$. Then there are $b' \in (\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex')^{-1}(p')$
          and $c \in q$ such that $b' \wedge' h(c) \leq a'$.
          We find
          $$
            \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex'b' \wedge h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c) = \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex'b' \wedge \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex' h(c) \leq \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a'.
          $$
          By construction we have $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex'b' \in p'$.
          Furthermore, $c \in q$ implies $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c \in h^{-1}(p')$
          and hence $h(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex c) \in p'$.
          Therefore $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a' \in p'$, and consequently
          $p'R'q'$.  \qedhere
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
The duality between $\mathsf{Lat}$ and $\mathsf{LSpace}$ lifts to a duality
  $$
    \mathsf{MLat} \equiv^{\operatorname{op}} \mathsf{MLSpace}.
  $$
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, \leq, \tau, R)$ be a modal L-space.
  This gives rise to the modal lattice
  $\mathbb{X}^* = (\mathcal{F}_{clp} \mathbb{X}, [R], \langle R \rangle)$,
  which in turn yields a modal L-space
  $(\mathbb{X}^*)_* = (\mathcal{F} \mathcal{F}_{clp} \mathbb{X}, \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}, \cap, \tau_{\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}}, R_{\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}})$.
  As a consequence of the duality for lattices (Theorem~\ref{thm:duality-lat-lspace})
  we know that $(X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau)$ is isomorphic to $(\mathcal{F}_b\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}, A, \cap, \tau_{\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}})$
  via $x \mapsto \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(x) = \{ a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X} \mid x \in a \}$.
  So we only have to show that $R$ and $R_{\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}}$ coincide.
  This can be seen as follows:
  \begin{align*}
    xRy
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \forall a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}
            \big( x \in [R]a \text{ implies } y \in a
                  \text{ and } y \in a \text{ implies } \langle R \rangle a \big) \\
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \forall a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}
            \big([R]a \in \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(x)
                 \text{ implies } a \in \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(y)
                 \text{ and } a \in \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(y)
                 \text{ implies } \langle R \rangle a \in \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(x) \big) \\
                                        
                                                    
                                                        
                                                  
                                                                                         
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \forall a \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}
            \big( [R]^{-1}(\eta_{\mathbb{X}}(x)) \subseteq \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(y)
            \subseteq \langle R \rangle^{-1}(\eta_{\mathbb{X}}(x)) \big) \\
      &\quad\text{iff}\quad \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(x) R_{\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}} \eta_{\mathbb{X}}(y)
  \end{align*}
  
  Next, let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice,
  $\mathscr{A}_* = (\mathcal{F} A, R_A)$ and
  $(\mathscr{A}_*)^* = (\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathcal{F} A, [R_A], \langle R_A \rangle)$.
  Then the duality for lattices from Theorem~\ref{thm:duality-lat-lspace}
  tells us that $A$ and $\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathcal{F} A$ are isomorphic via
  $a \mapsto \theta_A(a) = \{ p \in \mathcal{F} A \mid a \in p \}$,
  so we just have to show that $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex$ coincides with $[R_A]$ and
  $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex$ coincides with $\langle R_A \rangle$.
  That is, we have to show that $\theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a) = [R_A]\theta_A(a)$ and
  $\theta_A(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex a) = \langle R_A \rangle \theta_A(a)$.
  But we have already proven that in Lemma~\ref{lem:modal-lat-3}.
  
  The two paragraphs above establish the duality on objects.
  The duality for morphisms follows immediately from Theorem~\ref{thm:duality-lat-lspace}
  and the fact that all our categories are concrete.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- definition-30: body_md -->
Let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice.
  Let $i : A \to \operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(A)$ be the filter completion of $A$,
  and $j : A \to \operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}(A)$ the ideal completion.
                                                     
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item The \emph{filter completion} of $\mathscr{A}$ is the modal lattice
          $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A}) = (\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(A), \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}})$
          where
          \begin{align*}
            \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}} c
              &= \bigwedge \{ i(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} a) \mid a \in A \text{ and } c \leq i(a) \}
                 \qquad\text{for } \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \in \{ \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex \}.
                               
                                                                                     
                                   
                                                                                      
          \end{align*}
    \item The \emph{ideal completion} of $\mathscr{A}$ is the modal lattice
          $\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}(\mathscr{A}) = (\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}(A), \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}}, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}})$
          where
          \begin{align*}
            \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}^{\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}} c
              &= \bigvee \{ j(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} a) \mid a \in A \text{ and } j(a) \leq c \}
                 \qquad\text{for } \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \in \{ \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex \}.
                             
                                                                                   
                                 
                                                                                    
          \end{align*}
    \item The \emph{$\Pi_1$-completion} of $\mathscr{A}$ is $\operatorname{\mathcal{ie}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A}))$,
          i.e.~the composition of the filter and the ideal completion.
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-30 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:mod-comp4}
  Let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice and
  $\mathbb{X}$ its dual modal L-space.
  Then:
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A})$ is isomorphic to the modal lattice of
          closed filters of $\mathbb{X}$, i.e.~to $(\pi\mathbb{X})^{\ddagger}$;
    \item $\operatorname{\Pi_1}(\mathscr{A})$ is isomorphic to the modal lattice of
          filters of $\mathbb{X}$, i.e.~to $(\kappa\mathbb{X})^*$.
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Let $\mathbb{X} = (X, 1, \curlywedge, \tau, R_A)$ be the modal L-space dual
  to $\mathscr{A}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:lattice-completions} we can identify
  the filter extension of $A$ with the lattice of closed filters of
  $\mathbb{X}$ with inclusion $\theta : A \to \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X})$.
 
  In $\mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}$, the top, bottom and meet are given by $X$, $\{ 1 \}$
  and intersection, and the join of a collection of filters is the smallest
  filter containing their union.
 
  So we only have to verify that for all $c \in \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X})$ we
  have $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}c = [R_A]c$ and $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}c = \langle R_A \rangle c$.
  Thus compute
  \begin{align*}
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}c
                                                                               
                                                       
      &= \bigcap \big\{ \theta(\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex a) \mid a \in A, c \subseteq \theta(a) \big\}
         &\text{(Definition of $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}$)} \\
      &= \bigcap \big\{ [R_A]\theta(a) \mid a \in A, c \subseteq \theta(a) \big\}
         &\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:modal-lat-3})} \\
      &= \bigcap \big\{ [R_A]b \mid b \in \mathcal{F}_{clp}(\mathbb{X}), c \subseteq b \big\}
         &\text{($\theta$ is an iso from $A$ to $\mathcal{F}_{clp}(\mathbb{X})$)} \\
      &= [R_A]c.
         &\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:Rc})}
  \end{align*}
  Similarly we find $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}c = \langle R_A \rangle c$ for all
  $c \in \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X})$.

  For the second item we adopt a similar strategy.
  Using Proposition~\ref{prop:lattice-completions},
  we identify the $\Pi_1$-completion of $A$ with $\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{X})$,
  the filters of $\mathbb{X}$ with operators as in~\eqref{eq:Pi1-comp},
  and inclusion $\theta : A \to \mathcal{F}(\mathbb{X})$.
  We view the filter completion of $A$ as sitting inside the
  $\Pi_1$-completion, just like $\mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}) \subseteq \mathcal{F}(\mathbb{X})$.
  Then we already know that $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}c = [R_A]c$ for all
  closed filters, wherefore
  $$
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\Pi_1}}d
      = \operatorname{\bigtriangledown} \big\{ \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}c \mid c \in \operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(A), c \subseteq d \big\}
      = \operatorname{\bigtriangledown} \big\{ [R_A]c \mid c \in \mathcal{F}_k(\mathbb{X}), c \subseteq d \big\}
      = [R]d
  $$
  for all filters $d$. We use Lemma~\ref{lem:Rc} for the last equality.
  Similarly we find $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex^{\operatorname{\Pi_1}}d = \langle R_A \rangle d$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
The filter and $\Pi_1$-completion of a modal lattice are
  modal lattices themselves.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:mod-pres-filt}
  Let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice and
  $\varphi, \psi \in \mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$.
  Then
  $$
    \mathscr{A} \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi
    \quad\text{iff}\quad
    \operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A}) \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi.
  $$
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:mod-pres}
  Let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice,
  $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A})$ its filter completion,
  and $\sigma$ a valuation of the proposition letters for $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A})$.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \label{it:mod-pres1}
          If $a \in A$ and $\varphi \in \mathbf{L_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}}$ are such
          that $a \in \sigma(\varphi)$, then there exists a valuation
          $\theta$ for $\mathscr{A}$ such that $\theta(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for
          all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$ and $\theta(\varphi) \leq a$.
    \item \label{it:mod-pres2}
          If $\theta$ is a valuation for $\mathscr{A}$ such that
          $\theta(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all $p$              
          then $\theta(\varphi) \in \sigma(\varphi)$ for all $\varphi \in \mathbf{L_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}}$.
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
We start with the first statement. We proceed by induction on the
  structure of $\varphi$.
 
  If $\varphi = p \in \operatorname{Prop}$ then we set $\theta(p) = a$ and $\theta(q) = \top$
  for all other $q \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  If $\varphi = \top$ then $\theta(\top) = \top \in \{ \top \} = \sigma(\top)$,
  and if $\varphi = \bot$ then $\theta(\bot) = \bot \in A = \sigma(\bot)$.
  
  Suppose $\varphi = \varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2$.
  If $a \in \sigma(\varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2) = \sigma(\varphi_1) \mathbin{\triangledown} \sigma(\varphi_2)$
  then there are $a_1 \in \sigma(\varphi_1)$ and $a_2 \in \sigma(\varphi_2)$
  such that $a_1 \wedge a_2 \leq a$.
  The induction hypothesis then gives valuations $\theta_1, \theta_2$ for
  $\mathscr{A}$ such that $\theta_1(\varphi_1) \leq a_1$ and $\theta_2(\varphi_2) \leq a_2$,
  and such that $\theta_1(p) \in \sigma_1(p)$ and $\theta_2(p) \in \sigma_2(p)$
  for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  Let $\theta = \theta_1 \wedge \theta_2$.
  Since $\theta_i(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$, and $\sigma(p)$
  is a filter, we have $\theta(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  Then $\theta(\varphi_i) \leq \theta_i(\varphi_i)$ for $i \in \{ 1, 2 \}$,
  and we find
  $\theta(\varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2)
    = \theta(\varphi_1) \wedge \theta(\varphi_2)
    \leq \theta_1(\varphi_1) \wedge \theta_2(\varphi_2)
    \leq a_1 \wedge a_2 \leq a.
  $
  
  If $\varphi = \varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2$, then
  $a \in \sigma(\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2) = \sigma(\varphi_1) \cap \sigma(\varphi_2)$
  so there are valuations $\theta_1, \theta_2$ such that
  $\theta_1(\varphi_1) \leq a$ and $\theta_2(\varphi_2) \leq a$,
  and such that $\theta_1(p) \in \sigma_1(p)$ and $\theta_2(p) \in \sigma_2(p)$
  for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  Let again $\theta = \theta_1 \wedge \theta_2$. Then just as above
  $\theta(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$. Moreover, $\theta(\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2)
    = \theta(\varphi_1) \vee \theta(\varphi_2)
    \leq \theta_1(\varphi_1) \vee \theta_2(\varphi_2)
    \leq a \vee a = a.
  $
  
  Finally, let $\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \in \{ \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex \}$
  and $\varphi = \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \varphi_1$.
  Suppose $a \in \sigma(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \varphi_1)$. Then
  $$
    a \in \sigma(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \varphi_1)
      = \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}} \sigma(\varphi_1)
      = \bigwedge \{ {\uparrow}(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b) \mid b \in A, \sigma(\varphi_1) \leq {\uparrow} b \}
      = \bigwedge \{ {\uparrow}(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b) \mid b \in A, b \in \sigma(\varphi_1) \}.
  $$
  The last equality follows from the fact that filters are ordered by reverse
  inclusion, so that $\sigma(\varphi_1) \leq {\uparrow} b$ iff
  $\sigma(\varphi_1) \supseteq {\uparrow} b$ iff $b \in \sigma(\varphi_1)$.
                                                      
                                                                             
                             
  Since $\bigwedge$ in $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A})$ is defined as $\operatorname{\bigtriangledown}$,
  there exist $b_1, \ldots, b_n \in A$ such that $b_i \in \sigma(\varphi_1)$
  for all $i \in \{ 1, \ldots, n \}$ and
  $\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b_n \leq a$.
 
  The induction hypothesis implies that there exist valuations $\theta_1, \ldots, \theta_n$
  for $\mathscr{A}$ such that $\theta_i(\varphi_1) \leq b_i$
  and $\theta_i(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$ and $i \in \{ 1, \ldots, n \}$.
  Since $\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}$ is monotone we find
  $\theta_i(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\varphi_1) = \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \theta_i(\varphi_1) \leq \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b_i$ for all $i$.
  Letting $\theta = \theta_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \theta_n$ gives
  $\theta(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\varphi_1) \leq \theta_i(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\varphi_1) \leq \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b_i$ for all $i$,
  so that $\theta(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\varphi_1) \leq \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} b_n \leq a$,
  as desired.
  Since $\sigma(p)$ is a filter and $\theta_i(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all
  $i \in \{ 1, \ldots, n \}$ we find $\theta(p) \in \sigma(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  
  We prove the second item by induction on the structure of $\varphi$ as well.
  The base cases $\varphi = \top, \bot, p \in \operatorname{Prop}$ are routine.
  Suppose $\varphi = \varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2$. By induction we have
  $\theta(\varphi_1) \in \sigma(\varphi_1)$ and $\theta(\varphi_2) \in \sigma(\varphi_2)$.
  So
  $\theta(\varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2)
    = \theta(\varphi_1) \wedge \theta(\varphi_2)
    \in \sigma(\varphi_1) \mathbin{\triangledown} \sigma(\varphi_2)
    = \sigma(\varphi_1 \wedge \varphi_2).
  $
  If $\varphi = \varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2$, then with the same induction hypothesis
  we find $\theta(\varphi_i) \leq \theta(\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2)$ so
  $\theta(\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2) \in \sigma(\varphi_i)$ for $i \in \{ 1, 2 \}$.
  Therefore
  $\theta(\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2) \in \sigma(\varphi_1) \cap \sigma(\varphi_2) = \sigma(\varphi_1 \vee \varphi_2)$.
 
  Lastly suppose $\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \in \{ \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex \}$ and $\varphi = \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \varphi_1$.
  By the induction hypothesis we have $\theta(\varphi_1) \in \sigma(\varphi_1)$,
  so $\sigma(\varphi_1) \supseteq {\uparrow} \theta(\varphi_1)$ and since
  $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A})$ is ordered by reverse inclusion, it follows from
  the definition of $\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}$ that
  $\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}\sigma(\varphi_1) \supseteq {\uparrow} \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}} \theta(\varphi_1)$,
  hence
  $$
    \theta(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\varphi_1)
      = \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\theta(\varphi_1)
      \in \scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}^{\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}}\sigma(\varphi_1)
      = \sigma(\scalebox{.75}{\text{\faStarO}}\varphi_1)
  $$
  as desired.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
We have $\mathscr{A} \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ iff $\mathscr{A} \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \wedge \psi = \varphi$,
  so it suffices to focus on equalities. The proposition letters play
  the role of variables, and every valuation of the proposition letters
  to elements in $\mathscr{A}$
  extends to valuations for $\varphi$ and $\psi$ in the obvious way.
  
  So suppose $\mathscr{A} \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi = \psi$.
  Let $\sigma$ be any valuation for $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A})$ and $a \in A$.
  We aim to prove that $a \in \sigma(\varphi)$ if and only if $a \in \sigma(\psi)$.
  So suppose $a \in \sigma(\varphi)$.
  Then by Lemma~\ref{lem:mod-pres}\eqref{it:mod-pres1} we can find a
  valuation $\theta$ for $\mathscr{A}$ such that $\theta(\varphi) \leq a$.
  By assumption $\mathscr{A} \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi = \psi$, so
  $\theta(\psi) = \theta(\varphi) \leq a$,
  hence by Lemma~\ref{lem:mod-pres}\eqref{it:mod-pres2} we find
  $a \in \sigma(\psi)$.
  Similarly we can prove that $a \in \sigma(\psi)$ implies $a \in \sigma(\varphi)$
  so that $\sigma(\varphi) = \sigma(\psi)$.
  Since $\sigma$ is arbitrary, we conclude $\operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A}) \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi = \psi$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:preservation-modal-lat}
  Let $\mathscr{A} = (A, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex, \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex)$ be a modal lattice and
  $\varphi, \psi \in \mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$.
  Then
  $$
    \mathscr{A} \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi
    \quad\text{iff}\quad
    \operatorname{\mathcal{fe}}(\mathscr{A}) \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi
    \quad\text{iff}\quad
    \operatorname{\Pi_1}(\mathscr{A}) \mathrel{\kern.1ex\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.81ex, line width=.1ex, line cap=round, scale=1.1]
    {\draw (0ex,-.75ex) -- (0ex,.75ex);
     \draw (0ex,0ex) -- (1ex,0ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:235:.91ex);
     \draw (-.4ex,0ex) arc(180:125:.91ex);}$_{}$}}\kern.1ex \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi.
  $$
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:canonicity-modal}
  Any consequence pair $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ of $\mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$-formulae is
  $\Pi_1$-persistent.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:compl-modal}
  Let $\Gamma$ be a set of consequence pairs.
  Then the logic $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}(\Gamma)$ is sound and complete with
  respect to the following classes of frames:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item Modal L-spaces validating $\Gamma$;
    \item Principal modal L-frames validating $\Gamma$;
    \item Modal L-frames validating $\Gamma$.
  \end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Similar to the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:compl}.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:st1-modal}
  Let $\mathfrak{M}$ be a modal L-model, $w$ a state in $\mathfrak{M}$ and
  $\varphi$ an $\mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$-formula. Then
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $\mathfrak{M}, w \Vdash \varphi$ iff $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi)[w]$;
    \item $\mathfrak{M}, w \Vdash \varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$
          iff $\mathfrak{M}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{st}_x(\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi)[w]$.
  \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
For all modal L-frames $\mathfrak{X} = (X, \leq, R)$ and modal 
  consequence pairs $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$,
  $$
    \mathfrak{X} \Vdash \psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi
      \quad\text{iff}\quad \mathfrak{X}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{so}(\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi).
  $$
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:increasing-val-modal}
  Let $\mathfrak{X}$ be a modal L-frame and let $V$ and $V'$ be valuations for $\mathfrak{X}$
  such that $V(p) \subseteq V'(p)$ for all $p \in \operatorname{Prop}$.
  Then for all $\varphi \in \mathbf{L}_{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex\!\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex}$ we have $V(\varphi) \subseteq V'(\varphi)$.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- definition-31: body_md -->
A \emph{boxed atom} is a formula of the form
  $$
    \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex^n p := \underbrace{\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex \cdots \hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.6ex,-.6ex) rectangle (.6ex,.6ex);}}\kern.2ex}_{\text{$n$ times}} p,
  $$
  where $p$ is a proposition letter.
 
  A \emph{Sahlqvist antecedent} is a formula made from boxed atoms,
  $\top$ and $\bot$ by freely using $\wedge$, $\vee$ and $\hspace{.2ex}\text{ 
  \tikz[baseline=-.6ex, rounded corners=.01ex, rotate=45, line width=.12ex]
    {\draw (-.5ex,-.5ex) rectangle (.5ex,.5ex);}}\kern.2ex$.
  A modal consequence pair $\varphi \trianglelefteqslant \psi$ is called \emph{Sahlqvist} if
  $\varphi$ is a Sahlqvist antecedent (and $\psi$ is any formula).
<!-- end definition-31 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:modal-corr}
                                                                     
                                             
                                                                       
                                                          
  Any Sahlqvist modal consequence pair $\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ locally corresponds to
  a first-order formula with one free variable.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
By Lemma ... we have $\mathfrak{X}, w \Vdash \psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi$ if and only if
  $\mathfrak{X}^{\circ} \models \operatorname{so}(\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi)[w]$.
  As in Theorem~\ref{thm:corr}, our strategy for obtaining a first-order
  formula is to remove all second-order quantifiers from $\operatorname{so}(\psi \trianglelefteqslant \chi)[w]$.
 
  We assume that no two quantifiers bind the same variable.
  The case where $\psi = \top$ or $\bot$ can be handled as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:corr}. 
 
  Let $p_1, \ldots, p_n$ be the propositional variables occurring in
  $\psi$, and write $P_1, \ldots, P_n$ for their corresponding unary predicates.
  We assume that every proposition letter that occurs in $\chi$
  also occurs is $\psi$, for otherwise we may replace it by $\bot$ to
  obtain a formula which is equivalent in terms of validity on frames.
  
  \bigskip\noindent
  {\it Step 1.}
  Use equivalences of the form
  $$
    (\exists w(\alpha(w)) \wedge \beta) \leftrightarrow \exists w(\alpha(w) \wedge \beta),
    \qquad
    (\exists w(\alpha(w)) \vee \beta) \leftrightarrow \exists w(\alpha(w) \vee \beta),
  $$
  and
  $$
    (\exists w (\alpha(w)) \to \beta) \leftrightarrow\forall w(\alpha(w) \to \beta)
  $$
  to pull out all existential quantifiers that arise in $\operatorname{st}_x(\psi)$.
  Let $Y := \{ y_1, \ldots, y_m \}$ denote the set of (bound) variables that arise
  in the antecedent of the implication from the second-order translation.
  We end up with a formula of the form
  $$
                                                                            
                                                                              
                                                 
    \forall P_1 \cdots \forall P_n \forall y_1 \cdots \forall y_m
      \big( (\operatorname{ISFIL} \wedge \operatorname{AT} \wedge \operatorname{REL}) \to \operatorname{st}_x(\chi)\big)
  $$
  where
  \begin{itemize}
    \item $\operatorname{ISFIL}$ is a conjunction of formulae of the form $\mathsf{isfil}(P_i)$;
    \item $\operatorname{BOX\hyphen AT}$ is a conjunction of standard translations of boxed proposition letters,
          i.e.~formulae of the form $\forall z(z'R^nz \to P_iz)$
          (here $P_iz$ is viewed as $\forall z(z'R^0z \to P_iz)$);
    \item $\operatorname{REL}$ is a conjunction of formulae of the form $zRz'$ and $\mathsf{abovemeet}(z;, z', z'')$.
  \end{itemize}
                                        
                                                                         
                                                                                    
                                                                           
                                            
                                                        
                
                                                                       

                    
                 
                                                                      
                                                
                                                                           
                 
                                  
                                                         
               
                                                    
                                                   
                            
 
                    
                 
                                        
     
                                                     
                                                    
     
      
     
                                                         
                                                            
     
                                                                             
                                          
                                                                  
                                                           
                 
  
  \noindent
  {\it Step 2.} Next we read off minimal instances of the $P_i$ making the
  antecedent true.
                                                           
                                            
 
  For each proposition letter $P_i$, let $\forall y_{i_1}(z_{i_1}R^{n_1}y_{i_1} \to P_iy_{i_1}), \ldots, \forall y_{i_k}(z_{i_k}R^{n_k}y_{i_k} \to P_iy_{i_k})$ be
  the occurrences of $P_i$ in $\operatorname{BOX\hyphen AT}$ in the antecedent of \eqref{eq:post-process}.
  Intuitively, we define the valuation of $p_i$ to be the filter generated
  by the (interpretations of) $y_{i_1}, \ldots, y_{i_k}$.
  Formally,
  \begin{align*}
    \sigma(P_i) := 
      \bigvee \Big\{ &\exists w_{i_1} \cdots \exists w_{i_k}
        \big(z_{i_1}R^{n_1}w_{i_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge z_{i_k}R^{n_k}w_{i_k}  \\
        &\wedge \mathsf{abovemeet}(u; w_{i_1}, \ldots, w_{i_k})\big) \mid
         \{ i_1, \ldots, i_k \} \Big\}.
  \end{align*}
  (If $k = 0$, i.e.~there are no boxed atoms involving $P_i$ in the formula,
  then we take the empty meet to be falsum, i.e.~$\sigma(P_i) = \lambda u.(u \neq u)$.)
                                                                          
       
                                                                               
                                                                                
                                                                      
                                                                 
                                                   
  
  The remainder of the proof is analogous to the proof of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:corr}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
